What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling lately, and it's getting to the point where I don't know how to handle it. I'm a teaching assistant at the university, and usually, I love being on campus, you know? The energy, the students, the old brick buildings—I've always felt so connected to it, especially since I did my graduate work there. But lately... something is just very, very wrong. \n\nWhat scares me most—and this is why I finally made the appointment, because it's just so disturbing—is that when I walk across campus now, it feels completely different. It's like... it feels like I’m watching everything through glass. Like there's this invisible barrier between me and the rest of the world. I'll be walking to my seminar, and I feel like I’m not fully in my own body. It's the strangest sensation, almost like I'm floating just behind myself, watching myself walk. And the buildings and the students around me... they look slightly unreal. Like a movie set, or a photograph that's been altered just a little bit. It's terrifying, honestly, because I know where I am, but it doesn't *feel* real. I keep trying to shake it off, like blinking really hard or gripping the strap of my bag to ground myself, but it just stays like that. I just... I don't feel like myself, and the world doesn't feel like the world anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of detachment and the world seeming unreal?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let me think. It's been exactly six days. Yes, six days. Because it all started right after the showcase. We were having this big departmental showcase—it’s an annual thing we do where the students and faculty present their work, and I was helping coordinate the logistics for it in the main auditorium. And... well, during the event, the lighting rig... this huge, heavy metal lighting rig just collapsed right there in the middle of everything. \n\nIt was... I mean, the sound of it, the way it just came crashing down, it was horrific. But ever since that happened, exactly six days ago, I haven't been able to shake this feeling. I woke up the next morning and tried to go back to campus to help deal with the aftermath, and the moment I stepped onto the grounds, that glass wall feeling just dropped right over me. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last six days, from the minute I wake up and try to have my coffee to the minute I try to go to sleep, I'm just... floating. It’s like time stopped when that rig fell, and I’m just stuck in this weird, unreal echo of it. I keep thinking maybe tomorrow I'll wake up and feel solid again, but six days is a really long time to feel like you're not even in your own life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. I mean, absolutely, yes. I can't even... I can't even bring myself to go near the main building anymore. I used to walk right through the central courtyard, you know, right past the atrium where they held the showcase, because it's the fastest route to the humanities block where my office is. It's a beautiful walk usually, with the old oak trees and the fountain, but now... now I just can't do it. I've been taking this incredibly long, convoluted way around the entire perimeter of the campus just to avoid it. It adds like twenty minutes to my walk, and my bag is heavy, but the thought of even looking at those glass doors... it makes my chest tight. I just can't look at the space where it happened. \n\nAnd it's not just the place, either. It's the people, the conversations. The whole department is talking about it, obviously, because it was such a massive disaster and they're talking about safety reviews and insurance and all that. But the second someone brings up the showcase, or the rig, or even just asks how I'm doing regarding the whole mess, I just... I completely shut down. I'll just abruptly change the subject to something totally irrelevant, like the syllabus for next week, or I'll just make up an excuse and physically leave the room. Yesterday, one of the other TAs just mentioned the word 'auditorium' and I practically ran to the bathroom and hid in a stall for ten minutes. I just don't want to hear about it, I don't want to talk about it, I don't want to think about it. If I don't go near it and I don't talk about it, maybe I can just... I don't know, keep it pushed away. But it's exhausting, honestly, having to constantly navigate around my own workplace just to avoid any reminder of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares or flashbacks related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Well, the nightmares... yes, I've had a couple of dreams where I wake up sweating, but it's really the daytime that's the worst. It's not even a dream, it's just... it happens when I'm wide awake. I'll just be sitting at my kitchen island, you know, trying to grade papers for the intro course—which is already a nightmare because there are eighty of them and the handwriting is atrocious—or I'll just be standing at the counter making my morning coffee, pouring the water over the grounds, just totally normal, mundane things. And then, out of absolutely nowhere, the sound of that rig coming down just... it just barges right into my head. \n\nIt's so loud, doctor, it's like the metal is screeching inside my skull. And then it's not just the sound. It's like my vision just gets hijacked. Suddenly I'm not in my kitchen anymore. I can see the student volunteer who was standing near the stage—he was wearing this bright red sweater, I remember it so clearly—I can see him on the floor, and the dust kicking up, and the crowd of students just pushing forward in this wave of panic. And it feels... it feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again. My heart starts hammering, my hands shake so badly I spill the coffee or drop my red pen, and I'm just... I'm right back in that auditorium. It takes me minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize I'm actually safe in my own apartment. It's just so intrusive, like I have zero control over my own brain anymore. It just ambushes me when I least expect it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding talking or thinking about your feelings related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a minute ago, yes, absolutely. I am doing everything in my power to just... not go there. That's exactly why I've been taking that ridiculous, twenty-minute detour around the edge of campus so I don't have to pass the atrium. Because if I walk past those glass doors, I know I'll have to actually think about what happened, and if I think about it, I'll have to actually feel... whatever it is I'm feeling. Terror, I guess? I don't even really know, because I refuse to let myself dwell on it. \n\nAnd as for talking about it, like I mentioned with the other TAs, if anyone even hints at the showcase or asks how I'm coping, I just... I immediately change the subject or I literally just leave the room before I'm forced to talk about it. Just yesterday, Professor Higgins—he's the department chair, you know, very sweet older man but completely oblivious sometimes—he stopped me in the copy room and started asking how I was holding up emotionally after the 'incident,' as he called it. And I just... I panicked. I started talking at a million miles an hour about the midterm grading rubric for the undergrads. I just couldn't let him make me examine my feelings. \n\nI have to keep a lid on it, doctor. If I open that box and actually think about how scared I was, or how close I was to that falling metal, I don't know if I'll be able to close it again. So I just... I avoid the atrium, I avoid the conversations, I avoid the thoughts. It's like a full-time job just keeping it all pushed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... no, I don't think so. I mean, I've run through the whole thing in my head a thousand times—well, when I can't stop myself from thinking about it, anyway—and I just don't see how I could have done anything differently. I was just standing there, you know? I was just holding my clipboard with the schedule for the student presentations, waiting for the keynote speaker to finish up. I didn't set up the rigging. I didn't hire the contractors who put it together. I'm just a TA, I don't have any authority over the technical aspects of the showcase. \n\nI suppose... I mean, I guess if you really want to overthink it, maybe I could have noticed something looked off before it happened? But I'm not an engineer. I wouldn't know a faulty bolt from a perfectly fine one. And it happened so fast, there wasn't time to warn anyone or push anyone out of the way. One second everything was fine, the speaker was making a joke about post-modernism, and the next second it was just chaos. So, no, I don't feel guilty. I just feel... terrified. And exhausted from trying not to be terrified. But guilt? No, I don't think I'm carrying that particular burden, thank goodness. I have enough on my plate just trying to walk to my office without having a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... actually, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping. At least, not any more than usual. I mean, I'm a grad student and a TA, so my sleep schedule is already pretty chaotic. Between grading those eighty intro papers I mentioned, working on my own dissertation—which is on 19th-century epistolary novels, by the way, a fascinating topic but incredibly dense when you're trying to synthesize the research at 2 AM—and just the general anxiety of academia, I've never been someone who gets a solid eight hours. \n\nBut since the... since the incident with the rig, it hasn't really gotten worse in terms of just falling asleep or staying asleep. Like I said before, I've had a couple of those nightmares where I wake up sweating, but it's not like I'm lying awake for hours staring at the ceiling, unable to drift off. Once I finally put the red pen down, turn off the kitchen light, and actually get into bed, I usually crash pretty hard. I think I'm just so mentally and physically exhausted from spending the entire day trying to push away the memories and avoid the atrium and dodge conversations... by the time night rolls around, my brain just kind of shuts down in self-defense. \n\nSo, no, insomnia isn't really the issue. I'll sleep for six or seven hours, wake up to my alarm, and then... well, then the whole cycle of trying to keep the lid on the box starts all over again. But the actual sleeping part? That seems to be one of the few things my brain is still letting me do somewhat normally, thankfully. If I were sleep-deprived on top of everything else, I don't think I'd be able to function at all. I'd probably be making even less sense than I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats in your daily life since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Constantly on the lookout? No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm definitely avoiding the atrium, like I said, and I'm very careful about my route around campus. I take that long way by the old science building—the one with the gargoyles that desperately need power washing—just to make sure I don't even catch a glimpse of the glass doors. But it's not like I'm walking around looking up at the ceiling everywhere I go, expecting things to fall on me. \n\nI'm in my office, or the copy room, or the library, and those places feel fine. Well, fine in the sense that they aren't the atrium. I'm not scanning the room for structural weaknesses or checking the bolts on the bookshelves. I'm just... trying to grade papers. Trying to read about 19th-century letter-writing conventions. Trying to exist without thinking about the metal and the noise. \n\nSo, I wouldn't say I'm hyper-vigilant or whatever you'd call it. I'm not jumping at every creak the building makes. I'm just very, very specifically avoiding that one place and anything that reminds me of it. The rest of the time, I'm just trying to keep my head down and get through the day without having a breakdown in front of my undergrads. Which, let me tell you, is a challenge in itself when you're trying to explain the nuances of epistolary form to a group of nineteen-year-olds who would rather be anywhere else. But no, I'm not scanning for danger. I just know where the danger was, and I'm staying far, far away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Anxious most of the time? Um... no, actually. I wouldn't say most of the time. It's not like this constant, buzzing anxiety from the moment I wake up until I go to sleep. \n\nLike I was saying about my schedule, I just have so much to do, and when I'm actually doing it, I feel fine. I mean, when I'm sitting in my office in the humanities building—which is, thankfully, on the complete opposite side of campus from the student center—and I'm just focusing on my dissertation or trying to decipher the handwriting of a freshman who apparently doesn't believe in punctuation, I'm okay. I'm just... me. A slightly overworked, forty-two-year-old grad student trying to get by. \n\nI can really get lost in the research, you know? When I'm reading through those 19th-century letters, looking at the specific rhetorical devices they used to convey intimacy through correspondence... I'm completely absorbed in that world. I'm not sitting at my desk shaking or worrying about things falling on me or feeling panicky. The panic really only hits when I accidentally let my guard down and think about the showcase, or if I have to figure out a detour to avoid that side of campus. \n\nSo, if I can just keep my brain occupied with the 1800s and my students' essays, I actually feel pretty normal. It's just that keeping my brain perfectly occupied like that takes a tremendous amount of energy. But the anxiety itself? No, it's definitely not an all-day, every-day kind of thing. I think if I felt anxious *most* of the time, I would have had to take a leave of absence, and I really, really can't afford to do that right now, what with my funding being entirely tied to my TA position and my advisor already breathing down my neck for my next chapter. So, thankfully, no, it's not constant. Just... very intense when it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... it's not so much that I've consciously lost interest, or that I don't *want* to care about things. It's more like... I just can't actually *feel* the good parts of them right now? If that makes sense. \n\nLike, I still love my life, obviously, and my home, but there's this strange emotional disconnect that I don't know how to fix. For example, just the other day—I think it was Tuesday, because I had just finished teaching my afternoon seminar and came home completely drained—my husband, who has been incredibly patient and sweet through all of this, by the way... he went out to our garden. We have this lovely little patch of dahlias and zinnias that he's been cultivating all summer, which is no small feat with the terrible clay soil we have in our yard. \n\nAnyway, he cut some of the nicest flowers and arranged them in this little blue vase, and he brewed me a cup of my favorite Earl Grey tea. He brought it all into the study while I was sitting there staring blankly at my laptop screen. And... I looked at him, and I looked at the flowers, and intellectually, I *knew* it was such a kind, loving gesture. I knew it. I knew I should be smiling and feeling that warm, fuzzy appreciation that I normally would when he does something thoughtful like that. \n\nBut... nothing. I couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure at all. I just sat there, and inside, there was just this... this blank heaviness. Like someone had thrown a thick, wet wool blanket over all my emotions. I drank the tea, and I thanked him, of course, because I didn't want to hurt his feelings, but inside it was just completely flat. \n\nAnd it's like that with almost everything right now. The things that usually give me a little spark of joy, whether it's my husband's gardening, or a nice cup of tea, or even just reading a beautifully constructed sentence in one of my research books... the spark just isn't catching. I go through the motions of enjoying things, but the actual feeling of enjoyment is just buried under all that heavy, blank space.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Appetite or weight? Um... no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, my clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did a few weeks ago. I know a lot of people, when they go through something really... intense, they either completely lose their appetite because their stomach is just in knots, or they go the complete opposite direction and stress-eat everything in sight. Like, they'll just sit on the couch and demolish a whole sleeve of Oreos or a pint of ice cream just to feel something comforting. But I haven't been doing either of those things, really. 

My routine is pretty much exactly the same when it comes to food. I still get up and make my oatmeal with a little bit of brown sugar and some blueberries—well, if we have them, they've been ridiculously expensive at the grocery store lately, but I try to keep them around—and I eat that while I'm going over my lecture notes for the day. And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... I'm just completely drained. I don't have the energy to chop vegetables. So he'll make a stir-fry or bake some chicken, and I eat my normal portion. I might not be feeling that spark of joy about his cooking that I was talking about earlier, but I am still eating it.

I actually stepped on the scale just this past Sunday, mostly out of curiosity, because I was trying to figure out if I needed to go shopping for a new blazer for a conference I'm supposed to present at next month—assuming I can even get my act together to finish the paper for it, which is a big 'if' right now—and my weight was exactly the same. Almost down to the decimal point. So, yeah, no changes there at all. I'm just eating what I always eat and weighing what I always weigh.

And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... I'm just completely drained. I don't have the energy to chop vegetables. So he'll make a stir-fry or bake some chicken, and I eat my normal portion. I might not be feeling that spark of joy about his cooking that I was talking about earlier, but I am still eating it.\n\nI actually stepped on the scale just this past Sunday, mostly out of curiosity, because I was trying to figure out if I needed to go shopping for a new blazer for a conference I'm supposed to present at next month—assuming I can even get my act together to finish the paper for it, which is a big 'if' right now—and my weight was exactly the same. Almost down to the decimal point. So, yeah, no changes there at all. I'm just eating what I always eat and weighing what I always weigh.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I'm right back in that auditorium. It takes me minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize I'm actually safe in my own apartment. I'm just... me. A slightly overworked, forty-two-year-old grad student trying to get by. \n\nI can really get lost in the research, you know? I think if I felt anxious *most* of the time, I would have had to take a leave of absence, and I really, really can't afford to do that right now, what with my funding being entirely tied to my TA position and my advisor already breathing down my neck for my next chapter. We have this lovely little patch of dahlias and zinnias that he's been cultivating all summer, which is no small feat with the terrible clay soil we have in our yard. \n\nAnyway, he cut some of the nicest flowers and arranged them in this little blue vase, and he brewed me a cup of my favorite Earl Grey tea. I drank the tea, and I thanked him, of course, because I didn't want to hurt his feelings, but inside it was just completely flat. \n\nAnd it's like that with almost everything right now. The things that usually give me a little spark of joy, whether it's my husband's gardening, or a nice cup of tea, or even just reading a beautifully constructed sentence in one of my research books... the spark just isn't catching. I go through the motions of enjoying things, but the actual feeling of enjoyment is just buried under all that heavy, blank space.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Appetite or weight? Like, they'll just sit on the couch and demolish a whole sleeve of Oreos or a pint of ice cream just to feel something comforting. Other dangers? Um... no, not really. I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout for things like that. It's actually quite the opposite, as long as I'm nowhere near the student center. 

Like, I'm not walking down the street jumping at shadows, or constantly checking the ceiling every single place I go. Take the lecture hall where I teach my Tuesday/Thursday morning seminar, for instance. It's this incredibly old, drafty room down in the basement of the humanities building—probably built sometime in the late fifties or early sixties—and it has those terrible acoustic ceiling tiles. You know the ones? Half of them look like they have severe water damage, and the maintenance in that building is practically non-existent. We've actually had a work order in for a flickering fluorescent bulb near the whiteboard since October, and nobody has come to look at it. 

Honestly, if I were going to be paranoid about a ceiling collapsing or something falling on me, you'd think it would be in that basement, right? But when I walk in there to set up my slides, I'm not checking the structural integrity of the room or scanning for escape routes. I'm just annoyed that the projector takes ten minutes to warm up and I'm usually just reviewing my notes on Victorian prose. I'm completely oblivious to the room itself. 

And it's the same thing when I go to the grocery store, or when my husband drives us to the farmer's market on Sunday mornings. I'm not scanning the road for terrible accidents or worrying that a car is going to swerve into us. I'm usually just making a mental list of whether we need more oat milk or complaining about how much the price of eggs has gone up again. 

So, no, I'm not on edge about the whole world being dangerous. The world outside of that one specific building doesn't feel unsafe to me. If I'm away from the atrium, and I don't have to think about the showcase or the rig, my guard is completely down. I'm just my usual, distracted self, worrying about my dissertation deadlines and whether my undergrads actually did the assigned reading, rather than worrying about any actual physical danger. It really is just localized to that one awful spot on campus.

You know the ones? Half of them look like they have severe water damage, and the maintenance in that building is practically non-existent. We've actually had a work order in for a flickering fluorescent bulb near the whiteboard since October, and nobody has come to look at it. \n\nHonestly, if I were going to be paranoid about a ceiling collapsing or something falling on me, you'd think it would be in that basement, right? But when I walk in there to set up my slides, I'm not checking the structural integrity of the room or scanning for escape routes. I'm just annoyed that the projector takes ten minutes to warm up and I'm usually just reviewing my notes on Victorian prose. I'm completely oblivious to the room itself. \n\nAnd it's the same thing when I go to the grocery store, or when my husband drives us to the farmer's market on Sunday mornings. I'm not scanning the road for terrible accidents or worrying that a car is going to swerve into us. I'm usually just making a mental list of whether we need more oat milk or complaining about how much the price of eggs has gone up again. \n\nSo, no, I'm not on edge about the whole world being dangerous. The world outside of that one specific building doesn't feel unsafe to me. If I'm away from the atrium, and I don't have to think about the showcase or the rig, my guard is completely down. I'm just my usual, distracted self, worrying about my dissertation deadlines and whether my undergrads actually did the assigned reading, rather than worrying about any actual physical danger. It really is just localized to that one awful spot on campus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like, they'll just sit on the couch and demolish a whole sleeve of Oreos or a pint of ice cream just to feel something comforting. I know we sort of just touched on this a moment ago, but... no, not at all. Like I was saying about the grocery store and the farmer's market earlier, once I'm off campus, that feeling really just... evaporates. 

Take our neighborhood, for example. We live in this quiet little cul-de-sac about twenty minutes away from the university—it's one of those older subdivisions built in the late seventies, lots of mature oak trees that drop a ridiculous amount of leaves right around this time of year, which is a whole other headache because we're constantly having to rake them up. But when I'm walking around our block, or even if I have to run errands late at night...

Anyway, I pulled into this huge, practically empty parking lot under these towering streetlamps, and I wasn't looking over my shoulder. I wasn't scanning the roof of the drugstore or checking the automatic doors for some kind of danger. I wasn't checking for threats at all. I was just annoyed that I had to be out so late when I was already exhausted, and I was just hoping they hadn't reorganized the eye care aisle again because they do that every six months and it drives me crazy. 

So, no, honestly, I don't find myself checking my surroundings at all when I'm away from the school. My brain just... it doesn't even go there. The minute I drive off campus, I'm just my normal, easily-distracted self, worrying about completely mundane things like whether the self-checkout machine is going to freeze on me again. It really is just tied to that one building.

But I haven't been doing either of those things, really. \n\nMy routine is pretty much exactly the same when it comes to food. I still get up and make my oatmeal with a little bit of brown sugar and some blueberries—well, if we have them, they've been ridiculously expensive at the grocery store lately, but I try to keep them around—and I eat that while I'm going over my lecture notes for the day. And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... I'm just completely drained. I don't have the energy to chop vegetables. So he'll make a stir-fry or bake some chicken, and I eat my normal portion. I might not be feeling that spark of joy about his cooking that I was talking about earlier, but I am still eating it.\n\nI actually stepped on the scale just this past Sunday, mostly out of curiosity, because I was trying to figure out if I needed to go shopping for a new blazer for a conference I'm supposed to present at next month—assuming I can even get my act together to finish the paper for it, which is a big 'if' right now—and my weight was exactly the same. Almost down to the decimal point. So, yeah, no changes there at all. I'm just eating what I always eat and weighing what I always weigh.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for other potential dangers or threats besides the atrium?\n\nPatient: Other dangers? Um... no, not really. I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout for things like that. It's actually quite the opposite, as long as I'm nowhere near the student center. \n\nLike, I'm not walking down the street jumping at shadows, or constantly checking the ceiling every single place I go. Take the lecture hall where I teach my Tuesday/Thursday morning seminar, for instance. It's this incredibly old, drafty room down in the basement of the humanities building—probably built sometime in the late fifties or early sixties—and it has those terrible acoustic ceiling tiles. You know the ones? Half of them look like they have severe water damage, and the maintenance in that building is practically non-existent. We've actually had a work order in for a flickering fluorescent bulb near the whiteboard since October, and nobody has come to look at it. \n\nHonestly, if I were going to be paranoid about a ceiling collapsing or something falling on me, you'd think it would be in that basement, right? But when I walk in there to set up my slides, I'm not checking the structural integrity of the room or scanning for escape routes. I'm just annoyed that the projector takes ten minutes to warm up and I'm usually just reviewing my notes on Victorian prose. I'm completely oblivious to the room itself. \n\nAnd it's the same thing when I go to the grocery store, or when my husband drives us to the farmer's market on Sunday mornings. I'm not scanning the road for terrible accidents or worrying that a car is going to swerve into us. I'm usually just making a mental list of whether we need more oat milk or complaining about how much the price of eggs has gone up again. \n\nSo, no, I'm not on edge about the whole world being dangerous. The world outside of that one specific building doesn't feel unsafe to me. If I'm away from the atrium, and I don't have to think about the showcase or the rig, my guard is completely down. I'm just my usual, distracted self, worrying about my dissertation deadlines and whether my undergrads actually did the assigned reading, rather than worrying about any actual physical danger. It really is just localized to that one awful spot on campus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently checking your surroundings for potential threats or dangers when you're not on campus?\n\nPatient: I know we sort of just touched on this a moment ago, but... no, not at all. Like I was saying about the grocery store and the farmer's market earlier, once I'm off campus, that feeling really just... evaporates. \n\nTake our neighborhood, for example. We live in this quiet little cul-de-sac about twenty minutes away from the university—it's one of those older subdivisions built in the late seventies, lots of mature oak trees that drop a ridiculous amount of leaves right around this time of year, which is a whole other headache because we're constantly having to rake them up. But when I'm walking around our block, or even if I have to run errands late at night... \n\nLike, last Wednesday, I had to run out to the 24-hour pharmacy because I realized I was completely out of contact lens solution, and I absolutely cannot sleep in my contacts, my eyes get terribly dry and irritated if I do. Anyway, I pulled into this huge, practically empty parking lot under these towering streetlamps, and I wasn't looking over my shoulder. I wasn't scanning the roof of the drugstore or checking the automatic doors for some kind of danger. I wasn't checking for threats at all. I was just annoyed that I had to be out so late when I was already exhausted, and I was just hoping they hadn't reorganized the eye care aisle again because they do that every six months and it drives me crazy. \n\nSo, no, honestly, I don't find myself checking my surroundings at all when I'm away from the school. My brain just... it doesn't even go there. The minute I drive off campus, I'm just my normal, easily-distracted self, worrying about completely mundane things like whether the self-checkout machine is going to freeze on me again. It really is just tied to that one building.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly... yes. Yes, I am. And it’s awful, because I usually take my responsibilities as a TA very, very seriously. I mean, I’m forty-two, I’m not some twenty-two-year-old grad student who’s just here to socialize and skate by on the bare minimum. I really pride myself on being reliable. But lately... I just haven't been able to do my job normally at all. 

Like, just last week, we had our weekly planning meeting for the introductory literature course I help teach. We always hold it in this stuffy little conference room on the second floor, and usually, I’m the one taking all the notes, organizing the reading schedules, making sure we have the handouts ready for the week. But... I don't know, I was sitting there, and I just felt so overwhelmed and completely detached from what was happening. I couldn't focus on a single word Professor Miller was saying about the midterm grading rubric. I actually just stood up, right in the middle of him talking, grabbed my tote bag, and walked out. I didn't even give an excuse. I just left him and the other TAs sitting there staring at me. 

And it’s affecting the students, too, which is what really makes me feel terribly guilty. I’m supposed to do these one-on-one mentoring sessions for the undergrads who are struggling with their essays. I completely missed two of them this past week. I just didn't show up. One was for this really earnest freshman who just cannot grasp how to structure a thesis statement to save his life, and I had promised him I’d sit down with him for a full hour to go over his draft line by line. But the time came and I just... couldn't bring myself to go. 

A big part of the problem is that I’m just completely avoiding parts of campus that I’m actually supposed to use for my job. The student center, obviously, where the atrium is, but also the main administrative wing because it's attached to it by this glass skywalk. I'm supposed to drop off departmental paperwork there, and the coffee shop where I usually meet my mentees is right around the corner from that building, and I just won't go near any of it. I’ll walk twenty minutes out of my way in the freezing cold just to bypass that entire side of the quad, or, like with the mentoring sessions, I just won't go at all. So, yeah, I'm definitely dropping the ball on my responsibilities right now. It's becoming a really huge problem.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling lately, and it's getting to the point where I don't know how to handle it. I'm a teaching assistant at the university, and usually, I love being on campus, you know? The energy, the students, the old brick buildings—I've always felt so connected to it, especially since I did my graduate work there. But lately... something is just very, very wrong. \n\nWhat scares me most—and this is why I finally made the appointment, because it's just so disturbing—is that when I walk across campus now, it feels completely different. It's like... it feels like I’m watching everything through glass. Like there's this invisible barrier between me and the rest of the world. I'll be walking to my seminar, and I feel like I’m not fully in my own body. It's the strangest sensation, almost like I'm floating just behind myself, watching myself walk. And the buildings and the students around me... they look slightly unreal. Like a movie set, or a photograph that's been altered just a little bit. It's terrifying, honestly, because I know where I am, but it doesn't *feel* real. I keep trying to shake it off, like blinking really hard or gripping the strap of my bag to ground myself, but it just stays like that. I just... I don't feel like myself, and the world doesn't feel like the world anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this feeling of detachment and the world seeming unreal?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been... let me think. It's been exactly six days. Yes, six days. Because it all started right after the showcase. We were having this big departmental showcase—it’s an annual thing we do where the students and faculty present their work, and I was helping coordinate the logistics for it in the main auditorium. And... well, during the event, the lighting rig... this huge, heavy metal lighting rig just collapsed right there in the middle of everything. \n\nIt was... I mean, the sound of it, the way it just came crashing down, it was horrific. But ever since that happened, exactly six days ago, I haven't been able to shake this feeling. I woke up the next morning and tried to go back to campus to help deal with the aftermath, and the moment I stepped onto the grounds, that glass wall feeling just dropped right over me. And it hasn't stopped since. Every single day for the last six days, from the minute I wake up and try to have my coffee to the minute I try to go to sleep, I'm just... floating. It’s like time stopped when that rig fell, and I’m just stuck in this weird, unreal echo of it. I keep thinking maybe tomorrow I'll wake up and feel solid again, but six days is a really long time to feel like you're not even in your own life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any places, people, or activities since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. I mean, absolutely, yes. I can't even... I can't even bring myself to go near the main building anymore. I used to walk right through the central courtyard, you know, right past the atrium where they held the showcase, because it's the fastest route to the humanities block where my office is. It's a beautiful walk usually, with the old oak trees and the fountain, but now... now I just can't do it. I've been taking this incredibly long, convoluted way around the entire perimeter of the campus just to avoid it. It adds like twenty minutes to my walk, and my bag is heavy, but the thought of even looking at those glass doors... it makes my chest tight. I just can't look at the space where it happened. \n\nAnd it's not just the place, either. It's the people, the conversations. The whole department is talking about it, obviously, because it was such a massive disaster and they're talking about safety reviews and insurance and all that. But the second someone brings up the showcase, or the rig, or even just asks how I'm doing regarding the whole mess, I just... I completely shut down. I'll just abruptly change the subject to something totally irrelevant, like the syllabus for next week, or I'll just make up an excuse and physically leave the room. Yesterday, one of the other TAs just mentioned the word 'auditorium' and I practically ran to the bathroom and hid in a stall for ten minutes. I just don't want to hear about it, I don't want to talk about it, I don't want to think about it. If I don't go near it and I don't talk about it, maybe I can just... I don't know, keep it pushed away. But it's exhausting, honestly, having to constantly navigate around my own workplace just to avoid any reminder of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any nightmares or flashbacks related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Well, the nightmares... yes, I've had a couple of dreams where I wake up sweating, but it's really the daytime that's the worst. It's not even a dream, it's just... it happens when I'm wide awake. I'll just be sitting at my kitchen island, you know, trying to grade papers for the intro course—which is already a nightmare because there are eighty of them and the handwriting is atrocious—or I'll just be standing at the counter making my morning coffee, pouring the water over the grounds, just totally normal, mundane things. And then, out of absolutely nowhere, the sound of that rig coming down just... it just barges right into my head. \n\nIt's so loud, doctor, it's like the metal is screeching inside my skull. And then it's not just the sound. It's like my vision just gets hijacked. Suddenly I'm not in my kitchen anymore. I can see the student volunteer who was standing near the stage—he was wearing this bright red sweater, I remember it so clearly—I can see him on the floor, and the dust kicking up, and the crowd of students just pushing forward in this wave of panic. And it feels... it feels exactly like it's happening right in front of me all over again. My heart starts hammering, my hands shake so badly I spill the coffee or drop my red pen, and I'm just... I'm right back in that auditorium. It takes me minutes, sometimes longer, just to realize I'm actually safe in my own apartment. It's just so intrusive, like I have zero control over my own brain anymore. It just ambushes me when I least expect it.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding talking or thinking about your feelings related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a minute ago, yes, absolutely. I am doing everything in my power to just... not go there. That's exactly why I've been taking that ridiculous, twenty-minute detour around the edge of campus so I don't have to pass the atrium. Because if I walk past those glass doors, I know I'll have to actually think about what happened, and if I think about it, I'll have to actually feel... whatever it is I'm feeling. Terror, I guess? I don't even really know, because I refuse to let myself dwell on it. \n\nAnd as for talking about it, like I mentioned with the other TAs, if anyone even hints at the showcase or asks how I'm coping, I just... I immediately change the subject or I literally just leave the room before I'm forced to talk about it. Just yesterday, Professor Higgins—he's the department chair, you know, very sweet older man but completely oblivious sometimes—he stopped me in the copy room and started asking how I was holding up emotionally after the 'incident,' as he called it. And I just... I panicked. I started talking at a million miles an hour about the midterm grading rubric for the undergrads. I just couldn't let him make me examine my feelings. \n\nI have to keep a lid on it, doctor. If I open that box and actually think about how scared I was, or how close I was to that falling metal, I don't know if I'll be able to close it again. So I just... I avoid the atrium, I avoid the conversations, I avoid the thoughts. It's like a full-time job just keeping it all pushed down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the lighting rig collapse?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, I... no, I don't think so. I mean, I've run through the whole thing in my head a thousand times—well, when I can't stop myself from thinking about it, anyway—and I just don't see how I could have done anything differently. I was just standing there, you know? I was just holding my clipboard with the schedule for the student presentations, waiting for the keynote speaker to finish up. I didn't set up the rigging. I didn't hire the contractors who put it together. I'm just a TA, I don't have any authority over the technical aspects of the showcase. \n\nI suppose... I mean, I guess if you really want to overthink it, maybe I could have noticed something looked off before it happened? But I'm not an engineer. I wouldn't know a faulty bolt from a perfectly fine one. And it happened so fast, there wasn't time to warn anyone or push anyone out of the way. One second everything was fine, the speaker was making a joke about post-modernism, and the next second it was just chaos. So, no, I don't feel guilty. I just feel... terrified. And exhausted from trying not to be terrified. But guilt? No, I don't think I'm carrying that particular burden, thank goodness. I have enough on my plate just trying to walk to my office without having a panic attack.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, well... actually, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble sleeping. At least, not any more than usual. I mean, I'm a grad student and a TA, so my sleep schedule is already pretty chaotic. Between grading those eighty intro papers I mentioned, working on my own dissertation—which is on 19th-century epistolary novels, by the way, a fascinating topic but incredibly dense when you're trying to synthesize the research at 2 AM—and just the general anxiety of academia, I've never been someone who gets a solid eight hours. \n\nBut since the... since the incident with the rig, it hasn't really gotten worse in terms of just falling asleep or staying asleep. Like I said before, I've had a couple of those nightmares where I wake up sweating, but it's not like I'm lying awake for hours staring at the ceiling, unable to drift off. Once I finally put the red pen down, turn off the kitchen light, and actually get into bed, I usually crash pretty hard. I think I'm just so mentally and physically exhausted from spending the entire day trying to push away the memories and avoid the atrium and dodge conversations... by the time night rolls around, my brain just kind of shuts down in self-defense. \n\nSo, no, insomnia isn't really the issue. I'll sleep for six or seven hours, wake up to my alarm, and then... well, then the whole cycle of trying to keep the lid on the box starts all over again. But the actual sleeping part? That seems to be one of the few things my brain is still letting me do somewhat normally, thankfully. If I were sleep-deprived on top of everything else, I don't think I'd be able to function at all. I'd probably be making even less sense than I am right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats in your daily life since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Constantly on the lookout? No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm definitely avoiding the atrium, like I said, and I'm very careful about my route around campus. I take that long way by the old science building—the one with the gargoyles that desperately need power washing—just to make sure I don't even catch a glimpse of the glass doors. But it's not like I'm walking around looking up at the ceiling everywhere I go, expecting things to fall on me. \n\nI'm in my office, or the copy room, or the library, and those places feel fine. Well, fine in the sense that they aren't the atrium. I'm not scanning the room for structural weaknesses or checking the bolts on the bookshelves. I'm just... trying to grade papers. Trying to read about 19th-century letter-writing conventions. Trying to exist without thinking about the metal and the noise. \n\nSo, I wouldn't say I'm hyper-vigilant or whatever you'd call it. I'm not jumping at every creak the building makes. I'm just very, very specifically avoiding that one place and anything that reminds me of it. The rest of the time, I'm just trying to keep my head down and get through the day without having a breakdown in front of my undergrads. Which, let me tell you, is a challenge in itself when you're trying to explain the nuances of epistolary form to a group of nineteen-year-olds who would rather be anywhere else. But no, I'm not scanning for danger. I just know where the danger was, and I'm staying far, far away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Anxious most of the time? Um... no, actually. I wouldn't say most of the time. It's not like this constant, buzzing anxiety from the moment I wake up until I go to sleep. \n\nLike I was saying about my schedule, I just have so much to do, and when I'm actually doing it, I feel fine. I mean, when I'm sitting in my office in the humanities building—which is, thankfully, on the complete opposite side of campus from the student center—and I'm just focusing on my dissertation or trying to decipher the handwriting of a freshman who apparently doesn't believe in punctuation, I'm okay. I'm just... me. A slightly overworked, forty-two-year-old grad student trying to get by. \n\nI can really get lost in the research, you know? When I'm reading through those 19th-century letters, looking at the specific rhetorical devices they used to convey intimacy through correspondence... I'm completely absorbed in that world. I'm not sitting at my desk shaking or worrying about things falling on me or feeling panicky. The panic really only hits when I accidentally let my guard down and think about the showcase, or if I have to figure out a detour to avoid that side of campus. \n\nSo, if I can just keep my brain occupied with the 1800s and my students' essays, I actually feel pretty normal. It's just that keeping my brain perfectly occupied like that takes a tremendous amount of energy. But the anxiety itself? No, it's definitely not an all-day, every-day kind of thing. I think if I felt anxious *most* of the time, I would have had to take a leave of absence, and I really, really can't afford to do that right now, what with my funding being entirely tied to my TA position and my advisor already breathing down my neck for my next chapter. So, thankfully, no, it's not constant. Just... very intense when it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Lost interest... it's not so much that I've consciously lost interest, or that I don't *want* to care about things. It's more like... I just can't actually *feel* the good parts of them right now? If that makes sense. \n\nLike, I still love my life, obviously, and my home, but there's this strange emotional disconnect that I don't know how to fix. For example, just the other day—I think it was Tuesday, because I had just finished teaching my afternoon seminar and came home completely drained—my husband, who has been incredibly patient and sweet through all of this, by the way... he went out to our garden. We have this lovely little patch of dahlias and zinnias that he's been cultivating all summer, which is no small feat with the terrible clay soil we have in our yard. \n\nAnyway, he cut some of the nicest flowers and arranged them in this little blue vase, and he brewed me a cup of my favorite Earl Grey tea. He brought it all into the study while I was sitting there staring blankly at my laptop screen. And... I looked at him, and I looked at the flowers, and intellectually, I *knew* it was such a kind, loving gesture. I knew it. I knew I should be smiling and feeling that warm, fuzzy appreciation that I normally would when he does something thoughtful like that. \n\nBut... nothing. I couldn't feel the warmth or the pleasure at all. I just sat there, and inside, there was just this... this blank heaviness. Like someone had thrown a thick, wet wool blanket over all my emotions. I drank the tea, and I thanked him, of course, because I didn't want to hurt his feelings, but inside it was just completely flat. \n\nAnd it's like that with almost everything right now. The things that usually give me a little spark of joy, whether it's my husband's gardening, or a nice cup of tea, or even just reading a beautifully constructed sentence in one of my research books... the spark just isn't catching. I go through the motions of enjoying things, but the actual feeling of enjoyment is just buried under all that heavy, blank space.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Appetite or weight? Um... no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, my clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did a few weeks ago. I know a lot of people, when they go through something really... intense, they either completely lose their appetite because their stomach is just in knots, or they go the complete opposite direction and stress-eat everything in sight. Like, they'll just sit on the couch and demolish a whole sleeve of Oreos or a pint of ice cream just to feel something comforting. And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... I'm just completely drained. I don't have the energy to chop vegetables. So he'll make a stir-fry or bake some chicken, and I eat my normal portion. I might not be feeling that spark of joy about his cooking that I was talking about earlier, but I am still eating it.\n\nI actually stepped on the scale just this past Sunday, mostly out of curiosity, because I was trying to figure out if I needed to go shopping for a new blazer for a conference I'm supposed to present at next month—assuming I can even get my act together to finish the paper for it, which is a big 'if' right now—and my weight was exactly the same. Almost down to the decimal point. So, yeah, no changes there at all. I'm just eating what I always eat and weighing what I always weigh.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for other potential dangers or threats besides the atrium?\n\nPatient: Other dangers? Um... no, not really. I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout for things like that. It's actually quite the opposite, as long as I'm nowhere near the student center. \n\nLike, I'm not walking down the street jumping at shadows, or constantly checking the ceiling every single place I go. Take the lecture hall where I teach my Tuesday/Thursday morning seminar, for instance. It's this incredibly old, drafty room down in the basement of the humanities building—probably built sometime in the late fifties or early sixties—and it has those terrible acoustic ceiling tiles. You know the ones? Half of them look like they have severe water damage, and the maintenance in that building is practically non-existent. We've actually had a work order in for a flickering fluorescent bulb near the whiteboard since October, and nobody has come to look at it. \n\nHonestly, if I were going to be paranoid about a ceiling collapsing or something falling on me, you'd think it would be in that basement, right? But when I walk in there to set up my slides, I'm not checking the structural integrity of the room or scanning for escape routes. I'm just annoyed that the projector takes ten minutes to warm up and I'm usually just reviewing my notes on Victorian prose. I'm completely oblivious to the room itself. \n\nAnd it's the same thing when I go to the grocery store, or when my husband drives us to the farmer's market on Sunday mornings. I'm not scanning the road for terrible accidents or worrying that a car is going to swerve into us. I'm usually just making a mental list of whether we need more oat milk or complaining about how much the price of eggs has gone up again. \n\nSo, no, I'm not on edge about the whole world being dangerous. The world outside of that one specific building doesn't feel unsafe to me. If I'm away from the atrium, and I don't have to think about the showcase or the rig, my guard is completely down. I'm just my usual, distracted self, worrying about my dissertation deadlines and whether my undergrads actually did the assigned reading, rather than worrying about any actual physical danger. It really is just localized to that one awful spot on campus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently checking your surroundings for potential threats or dangers when you're not on campus?\n\nPatient: I know we sort of just touched on this a moment ago, but... no, not at all. We live in this quiet little cul-de-sac about twenty minutes away from the university—it's one of those older subdivisions built in the late seventies, lots of mature oak trees that drop a ridiculous amount of leaves right around this time of year, which is a whole other headache because we're constantly having to rake them up. I wasn't scanning the roof of the drugstore or checking the automatic doors for some kind of danger. I wasn't checking for threats at all. I was just annoyed that I had to be out so late when I was already exhausted, and I was just hoping they hadn't reorganized the eye care aisle again because they do that every six months and it drives me crazy. \n\nSo, no, honestly, I don't find myself checking my surroundings at all when I'm away from the school. My brain just... it doesn't even go there. It really is just tied to that one building.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble fulfilling your responsibilities as a teaching assistant or in other areas of your life because of how you've been feeling since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yes. I mean, I’m forty-two, I’m not some twenty-two-year-old grad student who’s just here to socialize and skate by on the bare minimum. I really pride myself on being reliable. But lately... But... I don't know, I was sitting there, and I just felt so overwhelmed and completely detached from what was happening. I couldn't focus on a single word Professor Miller was saying about the midterm grading rubric. I actually just stood up, right in the middle of him talking, grabbed my tote bag, and walked out. I didn't even give an excuse. I just left him and the other TAs sitting there staring at me. \n\nAnd it’s affecting the students, too, which is what really makes me feel terribly guilty. One was for this really earnest freshman who just cannot grasp how to structure a thesis statement to save his life, and I had promised him I’d sit down with him for a full hour to go over his draft line by line. The things that usually give me a little spark of joy, whether it's my husband's gardening, or a nice cup of tea, or even just reading a beautifully constructed sentence in one of my research books... the spark just isn't catching. I go through the motions of enjoying things, but the actual feeling of enjoyment is just buried under all that heavy, blank space.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Appetite or weight? Um... no, I haven't noticed anything like that. I mean, my clothes all still fit exactly the same as they did a few weeks ago. I know a lot of people, when they go through something really... intense, they either completely lose their appetite because their stomach is just in knots, or they go the complete opposite direction and stress-eat everything in sight. Like, they'll just sit on the couch and demolish a whole sleeve of Oreos or a pint of ice cream just to feel something comforting. But I haven't been doing either of those things, really. \n\nMy routine is pretty much exactly the same when it comes to food. I still get up and make my oatmeal with a little bit of brown sugar and some blueberries—well, if we have them, they've been ridiculously expensive at the grocery store lately, but I try to keep them around—and I eat that while I'm going over my lecture notes for the day. And then for dinner, my husband is usually the one who cooks, because by the time I get home from teaching and holding my afternoon office hours... which, honestly, is just me sitting in a freezing little cubicle for two hours waiting for undergrads who only ever show up the day before a midterm... I'm just completely drained. I don't have the energy to chop vegetables. So he'll make a stir-fry or bake some chicken, and I eat my normal portion. I might not be feeling that spark of joy about his cooking that I was talking about earlier, but I am still eating it.\n\nI actually stepped on the scale just this past Sunday, mostly out of curiosity, because I was trying to figure out if I needed to go shopping for a new blazer for a conference I'm supposed to present at next month—assuming I can even get my act together to finish the paper for it, which is a big 'if' right now—and my weight was exactly the same. Almost down to the decimal point. So, yeah, no changes there at all. I'm just eating what I always eat and weighing what I always weigh.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for other potential dangers or threats besides the atrium?\n\nPatient: Other dangers? Um... no, not really. I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout for things like that. It's actually quite the opposite, as long as I'm nowhere near the student center. \n\nLike, I'm not walking down the street jumping at shadows, or constantly checking the ceiling every single place I go. Take the lecture hall where I teach my Tuesday/Thursday morning seminar, for instance. It's this incredibly old, drafty room down in the basement of the humanities building—probably built sometime in the late fifties or early sixties—and it has those terrible acoustic ceiling tiles. You know the ones? Half of them look like they have severe water damage, and the maintenance in that building is practically non-existent. We've actually had a work order in for a flickering fluorescent bulb near the whiteboard since October, and nobody has come to look at it. \n\nHonestly, if I were going to be paranoid about a ceiling collapsing or something falling on me, you'd think it would be in that basement, right? But when I walk in there to set up my slides, I'm not checking the structural integrity of the room or scanning for escape routes. I'm just annoyed that the projector takes ten minutes to warm up and I'm usually just reviewing my notes on Victorian prose. I'm completely oblivious to the room itself. \n\nAnd it's the same thing when I go to the grocery store, or when my husband drives us to the farmer's market on Sunday mornings. I'm not scanning the road for terrible accidents or worrying that a car is going to swerve into us. I'm usually just making a mental list of whether we need more oat milk or complaining about how much the price of eggs has gone up again. \n\nSo, no, I'm not on edge about the whole world being dangerous. The world outside of that one specific building doesn't feel unsafe to me. If I'm away from the atrium, and I don't have to think about the showcase or the rig, my guard is completely down. I'm just my usual, distracted self, worrying about my dissertation deadlines and whether my undergrads actually did the assigned reading, rather than worrying about any actual physical danger. It really is just localized to that one awful spot on campus.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself frequently checking your surroundings for potential threats or dangers when you're not on campus?\n\nPatient: I know we sort of just touched on this a moment ago, but... no, not at all. Like I was saying about the grocery store and the farmer's market earlier, once I'm off campus, that feeling really just... evaporates. \n\nTake our neighborhood, for example. We live in this quiet little cul-de-sac about twenty minutes away from the university—it's one of those older subdivisions built in the late seventies, lots of mature oak trees that drop a ridiculous amount of leaves right around this time of year, which is a whole other headache because we're constantly having to rake them up. But when I'm walking around our block, or even if I have to run errands late at night... \n\nLike, last Wednesday, I had to run out to the 24-hour pharmacy because I realized I was completely out of contact lens solution, and I absolutely cannot sleep in my contacts, my eyes get terribly dry and irritated if I do. Anyway, I pulled into this huge, practically empty parking lot under these towering streetlamps, and I wasn't looking over my shoulder. I wasn't scanning the roof of the drugstore or checking the automatic doors for some kind of danger. I wasn't checking for threats at all. I was just annoyed that I had to be out so late when I was already exhausted, and I was just hoping they hadn't reorganized the eye care aisle again because they do that every six months and it drives me crazy. \n\nSo, no, honestly, I don't find myself checking my surroundings at all when I'm away from the school. My brain just... it doesn't even go there. The minute I drive off campus, I'm just my normal, easily-distracted self, worrying about completely mundane things like whether the self-checkout machine is going to freeze on me again. It really is just tied to that one building.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble fulfilling your responsibilities as a teaching assistant or in other areas of your life because of how you've been feeling since the lighting rig collapsed?\n\nPatient: Honestly... yes. Yes, I am. And it’s awful, because I usually take my responsibilities as a TA very, very seriously. I mean, I’m forty-two, I’m not some twenty-two-year-old grad student who’s just here to socialize and skate by on the bare minimum. I really pride myself on being reliable. But lately... I just haven't been able to do my job normally at all. \n\nLike, just last week, we had our weekly planning meeting for the introductory literature course I help teach. We always hold it in this stuffy little conference room on the second floor, and usually, I’m the one taking all the notes, organizing the reading schedules, making sure we have the handouts ready for the week. But... I don't know, I was sitting there, and I just felt so overwhelmed and completely detached from what was happening. I couldn't focus on a single word Professor Miller was saying about the midterm grading rubric. I actually just stood up, right in the middle of him talking, grabbed my tote bag, and walked out. I didn't even give an excuse. I just left him and the other TAs sitting there staring at me. \n\nAnd it’s affecting the students, too, which is what really makes me feel terribly guilty. I’m supposed to do these one-on-one mentoring sessions for the undergrads who are struggling with their essays. I completely missed two of them this past week. I just didn't show up. One was for this really earnest freshman who just cannot grasp how to structure a thesis statement to save his life, and I had promised him I’d sit down with him for a full hour to go over his draft line by line. But the time came and I just... couldn't bring myself to go. \n\nA big part of the problem is that I’m just completely avoiding parts of campus that I’m actually supposed to use for my job. The student center, obviously, where the atrium is, but also the main administrative wing because it's attached to it by this glass skywalk. I'm supposed to drop off departmental paperwork there, and the coffee shop where I usually meet my mentees is right around the corner from that building, and I just won't go near any of it. I’ll walk twenty minutes out of my way in the freezing cold just to bypass that entire side of the quad, or, like with the mentoring sessions, I just won't go at all. So, yeah, I'm definitely dropping the ball on my responsibilities right now. It's becoming a really huge problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, absolutely. It’s actually becoming incredibly exhausting, to be honest. I know I mentioned a minute ago that I avoid the student center, but it's... it's to the point where I’ve been taking the absolute longest way around campus just so I don’t even have to pass anywhere near the atrium. 

Normally, I would just cut right through the main plaza—it’s this wide, brick-paved walkway that takes maybe five minutes to cross from the library to the humanities building. But lately? I’m walking all the way down University Avenue, past the old botanical gardens, looping entirely around the visitor parking structures... just to get to my office. It easily adds twenty or twenty-five minutes to my walk, and with the wind chill we've been having this week, it is absolutely miserable. My winter coat really isn't meant for that kind of trek, and I end up getting to my desk freezing and completely out of breath. But I just can't risk even seeing those glass doors from a distance. I can't even look at that side of the campus.

And it’s not just the physical building I'm avoiding, either. It’s... well, the spring arts showcase is practically all anyone in the department wants to gossip about right now, given what happened. I’ll be in the graduate lounge, just trying to heat up my lunch in the microwave, and one of the other TAs or a faculty member will bring up the showcase, or the lighting rig, or how the theater department is scrambling to reschedule. And the second they do, my chest just tightens up and I have to shut it down. I’ll aggressively change the subject to something completely unrelated, like the upcoming syllabus revisions or the copy machine in the main office being jammed again. 

Or, if they keep talking about it and won't take the hint, I just leave. I've literally grabbed my Tupperware out of the microwave before the timer even went off and just walked right out of the room. I just leave before I have to talk about it or hear another word about it. People probably think I'm being incredibly rude, just walking out mid-conversation like that, but I can't help it. I just cannot hear about that showcase.
